</div>
</div></section></section><section aria-label="Article content"><section class="body main-article-body"><section class="abstract" id="abstract1"><h2>Abstract</h2>
<p>Tobacco misuse as a comorbidity of schizophrenia is frequently established during adolescence. However, comorbidity markers are still missing. Here, the method of label-free proteomics was used to identify deregulated proteins in the medial prefrontal cortex (prelimbic and infralimbic) of male and female mice modelled to schizophrenia with a history of nicotine exposure during adolescence. Phencyclidine (PCP), used to model schizophrenia (SCHZ), was combined with an established model of nicotine minipump infusions (NIC). The combined insults led to worse outcomes than each insult separately when considering the absolute number of deregulated proteins and that of exclusively deregulated ones. Partially shared Reactome pathways between sexes and between PCP, NIC and PCPNIC groups indicate functional overlaps. Distinctively, proteins differentially expressed exclusively in PCPNIC mice reveal unique effects associated with the comorbidity model. Interactome maps of these proteins identified sex-selective subnetworks, within which some proteins stood out: for females, peptidyl-prolyl cis-trans isomerase (Fkbp1a) and heat shock 70 kDa protein 1B (Hspa1b), both components of the oxidative stress subnetwork, and gamma-enolase (Eno2), a component of the energy metabolism subnetwork; and for males, amphiphysin (Amph), a component of the synaptic transmission subnetwork. These are proposed to be further investigated and validated as markers of the combined insult during adolescence.</p>
<section id="kwd-group1" class="kwd-group"><p><strong>Keywords:</strong> psychiatric disorder, nicotine addiction, proteome, E-cigarette, mass spectrometry, prodrome, co-exposure, comorbidity</p></section></section><section id="sec1-ijms-24-14634"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">1. Introduction</h2>
<p>Schizophrenia (SCHZ) is a devastating psychiatric disorder that impacts not only patients but also their families and society. Its occurrence is associated with a broad range of adverse outcomes, including but not limited to cognitive decline, social isolation, stigma, unemployment and reduced life expectancy [<a href="#B1-ijms-24-14634" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B1-ijms-24-14634">1</a>,<a href="#B2-ijms-24-14634" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B2-ijms-24-14634">2</a>]. The available pharmacological approaches to treat this disorder are largely based on the manipulation of neurotransmitter system activities, and their partial efficacy has fueled the development and acceptance of the main hypotheses of SCHZ [<a href="#B3-ijms-24-14634" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B3-ijms-24-14634">3</a>,<a href="#B4-ijms-24-14634" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B4-ijms-24-14634">4</a>]. Corroborating these hypotheses, alterations in several neurotransmitter systems were identified in SCHZ patients [<a href="#B5-ijms-24-14634" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B5-ijms-24-14634">5</a>] and in animal models [<a href="#B6-ijms-24-14634" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B6-ijms-24-14634">6</a>]. However, the effectiveness of antipsychotics is limited [<a href="#B7-ijms-24-14634" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B7-ijms-24-14634">7</a>], which suggests the involvement of other systems and pathways in the etiology and pathophysiology of this disorder. In this regard, recent studies showed significant changes in the expression of proteins not only related to synaptic signaling [<a href="#B8-ijms-24-14634" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B8-ijms-24-14634">8</a>] but also involved with functions and pathways such as oxidative stress responses, the immune system, mitochondrial and peroxisomal function, the cytoskeleton, protein trafficking, energy/mitochondrial metabolism and clathrin-mediated endocytosis in SCHZ patients [<a href="#B9-ijms-24-14634" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B9-ijms-24-14634">9</a>]. Corresponding findings were identified in animal models [<a href="#B10-ijms-24-14634" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B10-ijms-24-14634">10</a>].</p>
<p>Two factors add extra layers of complexity to the identification of the mechanisms involved in this mental disorder. One of them is gender. In this regard, there is a higher prevalence and severity of negative and cognitive symptoms as well as worse response to treatment with antipsychotic medication in men, and the biological elements that underlie these differences are underinvestigated [<a href="#B11-ijms-24-14634" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B11-ijms-24-14634">11</a>,<a href="#B12-ijms-24-14634" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B12-ijms-24-14634">12</a>,<a href="#B13-ijms-24-14634" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B13-ijms-24-14634">13</a>,<a href="#B14-ijms-24-14634" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B14-ijms-24-14634">14</a>,<a href="#B15-ijms-24-14634" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B15-ijms-24-14634">15</a>]. The second factor is the patients’ high vulnerability to substance use disorders, including nicotine addiction. The prevalence of conventional tobacco use in SCHZ patients is two to four times higher than the prevalence in individuals without a mental disorder, so that 50–90% of SCHZ patients smoke [<a href="#B16-ijms-24-14634" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B16-ijms-24-14634">16</a>,<a href="#B17-ijms-24-14634" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B17-ijms-24-14634">17</a>]. There was some speculation that nicotine potentiates psychosis [<a href="#B18-ijms-24-14634" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B18-ijms-24-14634">18</a>], but some studies failed to corroborate these findings [<a href="#B19-ijms-24-14634" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B19-ijms-24-14634">19</a>]. There is also evidence of procognitive effects of nicotine, improvement in the negative symptomatology [<a href="#B20-ijms-24-14634" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B20-ijms-24-14634">20</a>] and mitigation of antipsychotic side effects [<a href="#B21-ijms-24-14634" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B21-ijms-24-14634">21</a>]. Nevertheless, it is worth mentioning that smoking is a major factor that contributes to high mortality levels and reduced life expectancy in SCHZ patients [<a href="#B22-ijms-24-14634" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B22-ijms-24-14634">22</a>]. The binding of nicotine to nicotinic cholinergic receptors (nAChRs) not only has a profound impact on the cholinergic system but also, indirectly, modifies the functioning of other neurotransmitter systems [<a href="#B23-ijms-24-14634" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B23-ijms-24-14634">23</a>,<a href="#B24-ijms-24-14634" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B24-ijms-24-14634">24</a>], several of which are involved in the SCHZ pathophysiology [<a href="#B25-ijms-24-14634" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B25-ijms-24-14634">25</a>,<a href="#B26-ijms-24-14634" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B26-ijms-24-14634">26</a>]. However, so far, the mechanisms that underlie nicotine effects on SCHZ symptomatology are not established. This could be due to the involvement of pathways other than those associated with the classic neurotransmitter systems. In support of this possibility, there is evidence that, in smokers and in animal models, nicotine affects not only synaptic transmission but also multiple pathways and biological processes, and several of those are also reported to be enriched in SCHZ proteomic studies. These include energy/mitochondrial metabolism, oxidative stress response, clathrin-mediated endocytosis and the cytoskeleton [<a href="#B27-ijms-24-14634" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B27-ijms-24-14634">27</a>,<a href="#B28-ijms-24-14634" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B28-ijms-24-14634">28</a>,<a href="#B29-ijms-24-14634" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B29-ijms-24-14634">29</a>].</p>
<p>Frequently, attenuated positive, cognitive and negative symptoms of SCHZ are already observed before its diagnosis, including during adolescence [<a href="#B30-ijms-24-14634" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B30-ijms-24-14634">30</a>,<a href="#B31-ijms-24-14634" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B31-ijms-24-14634">31</a>,<a href="#B32-ijms-24-14634" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B32-ijms-24-14634">32</a>]. These events coincide with experimentation and progression to misuse of both conventional combustible tobacco products [<a href="#B33-ijms-24-14634" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B33-ijms-24-14634">33</a>] and the more recently available electronic nicotine delivery systems [<a href="#B34-ijms-24-14634" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B34-ijms-24-14634">34</a>,<a href="#B35-ijms-24-14634" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B35-ijms-24-14634">35</a>]. In accordance with these data, the prevalence of tobacco use in the prodromal phase of SCHZ was recently reported to vary from 17 to 46% [<a href="#B36-ijms-24-14634" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B36-ijms-24-14634">36</a>] and from 59 to 78% in patients with first-episode psychosis when compared with healthy matched controls [<a href="#B37-ijms-24-14634" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B37-ijms-24-14634">37</a>,<a href="#B38-ijms-24-14634" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B38-ijms-24-14634">38</a>]. These findings indicate that the association between SCHZ and nicotine misuse is established early. They further point to adolescence as a critical period for both nicotine misuse and SCHZ and also as a window of opportunity for early intervention [<a href="#B39-ijms-24-14634" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B39-ijms-24-14634">39</a>]. In this sense, the identification of pathways and biomarkers of this comorbidity’s early establishment could provide valuable information.</p>
<p>The medial prefrontal cortex (mPFC) is key to this investigation because it continues to mature during adolescence [<a href="#B40-ijms-24-14634" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B40-ijms-24-14634">40</a>]. Also, this brain region plays roles in executive functions such as decision making, attention, working memory and cognitive flexibility [<a href="#B41-ijms-24-14634" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B41-ijms-24-14634">41</a>] as well as social [<a href="#B42-ijms-24-14634" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B42-ijms-24-14634">42</a>] and addictive behaviors [<a href="#B43-ijms-24-14634" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B43-ijms-24-14634">43</a>], all of which are intimately associated with the symptomatology of SCHZ [<a href="#B2-ijms-24-14634" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B2-ijms-24-14634">2</a>,<a href="#B44-ijms-24-14634" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B44-ijms-24-14634">44</a>] and with mechanisms of nicotine addiction [<a href="#B45-ijms-24-14634" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B45-ijms-24-14634">45</a>,<a href="#B46-ijms-24-14634" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B46-ijms-24-14634">46</a>]. Despite that, there are scant studies in human subjects focused on the mPFC during this period of comorbid susceptibility. In addition to the limited access to brain samples, ethical concerns and difficulties in the control of confounding factors most likely have hampered advances in this area [<a href="#B47-ijms-24-14634" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B47-ijms-24-14634">47</a>,<a href="#B48-ijms-24-14634" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B48-ijms-24-14634">48</a>].</p>
<p>Although the association between SCHZ and nicotine misuse is frequent, smoking is seldom treated as a potential source of variation in epidemiological studies that use SCHZ subjects, and there is limited evaluation of its contribution to the outcomes. In this regard, the use of animal models may be of translational relevance. Phencyclidine, a noncompetitive N-methyl-D-aspartate (NMDA) receptor antagonist, is a dissociative drug that, in the period between the 1960s and the 1990s, was highly used as a drug of abuse under the street names of “Angel Dust” or “Peace Pill”. Interestingly, since its early use, phencyclidine has been described as a schizophrenomimetic drug because in healthy individuals, it triggers symptoms akin to those seen in SCHZ, while in SCHZ patients, the symptoms are worsened [<a href="#B49-ijms-24-14634" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B49-ijms-24-14634">49</a>]. Nowadays, phencyclidine administration is a validated and widely used SCHZ model [<a href="#B50-ijms-24-14634" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B50-ijms-24-14634">50</a>,<a href="#B51-ijms-24-14634" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B51-ijms-24-14634">51</a>] as its repeated administration in adulthood produces behavioral alterations reminiscent of positive, negative and cognitive symptoms of this disorder [<a href="#B52-ijms-24-14634" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B52-ijms-24-14634">52</a>]. This substance evokes deficits in prepulse inhibition [<a href="#B53-ijms-24-14634" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B53-ijms-24-14634">53</a>] and neurochemical dysfunctions that correlate with those identified in SCHZ subjects [<a href="#B54-ijms-24-14634" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B54-ijms-24-14634">54</a>]. These include hyperactivity in the mesolimbic dopaminergic system and hypoactivity in the mesocortical one, as well as hypofunction in the cortical glutamatergic and gamma aminobutyric acid (GABA) systems [<a href="#B50-ijms-24-14634" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B50-ijms-24-14634">50</a>,<a href="#B52-ijms-24-14634" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B52-ijms-24-14634">52</a>,<a href="#B54-ijms-24-14634" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B54-ijms-24-14634">54</a>]. Most importantly, phencyclidine-induced SCHZ-like behavioral deficits were recently also demonstrated to occur when administration occurs during adolescence [<a href="#B55-ijms-24-14634" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B55-ijms-24-14634">55</a>]. As for nicotine exposure, a subcutaneous osmotic minipump is an established model that has the advantage of being less stressful than other routes of administration, in addition to allowing for a constant dose and a controlled period of administration [<a href="#B56-ijms-24-14634" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B56-ijms-24-14634">56</a>,<a href="#B57-ijms-24-14634" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B57-ijms-24-14634">57</a>].</p>
<p>Proteomic approaches are useful for providing a broad view of the molecular alterations associated with SCHZ [<a href="#B9-ijms-24-14634" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B9-ijms-24-14634">9</a>,<a href="#B58-ijms-24-14634" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B58-ijms-24-14634">58</a>], which is also true for nicotine misuse [<a href="#B29-ijms-24-14634" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B29-ijms-24-14634">29</a>]. However, much less is known regarding the comorbidity, particularly during adolescence. Here, we used a label-free quantitative proteomics approach to identify deregulated proteins in the mPFC of adolescent male and female mice modelled to SCHZ with and without a history of nicotine administration. With that, we aimed to preclinically delineate factors involved in the comorbidity during the early course of its establishment and to investigate whether there are relevant sex differences in these factors. We hypothesized that the analysis of the mPFC would lead to the identification of key pathological markers of the combined insult, which would correspond to exclusively deregulated proteins in mice modelled to SCHZ and exposed to nicotine.</p></section><section id="sec2-ijms-24-14634"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">2. Results</h2>
<p>A total of 3594 unique peptides corresponding to 337 proteins were identified using a minimum of 2 peptides per protein that were present in at least two of the replicate analyses. A list of peptides and an alphabetized list containing identifiers for all mPFC proteins are available in <a href="#app1-ijms-24-14634" class="usa-link">Supplementary Material S1—Sheets 1 and 2</a>. An abundance ranking was also assigned to these proteins relative to their respective total independent spectra.</p>
<p>Considering the relevant sex differences in prevalence, symptom domains and severity described in SCHZ patients as well as animal models [<a href="#B59-ijms-24-14634" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B59-ijms-24-14634">59</a>], for the analyses, mice were a priori separated by sex. Approximately 49.8% of proteins were differentially expressed only in one sex, and there were differences in the direction of effects (upregulation or downregulation) in males and females for the different comparisons described throughout the results section, which validated our approach (<a href="#app1-ijms-24-14634" class="usa-link">Supplementary Material S1</a>). Also, for both males and females, changes in protein abundance depended on the drug(s) mice were exposed to, which corroborates the importance of analyzing independent factors (phencyclidine/nicotine). Firstly, PCP, NIC and PCPNIC were compared to CT mice with the aim of identifying the profile of deregulated proteins for each experimental group. Secondly, each group of mice exposed to phencyclidine (PCP and PCPNIC) was compared to the NIC group to isolate and subtract the effects of nicotine history per se. Thirdly, PCP and PCPNIC groups were compared to isolate proteins deregulated by phencyclidine and the combined insult. Next, to identify exclusive markers of PCP and NIC insults, the proteins commonly deregulated in all comparisons involving PCP (PCP vs. CT, PCP vs. NIC and PCP vs. PCPNIC) and NIC (NIC vs. CT, NIC vs. PCP and NIC vs. PCPNIC) were identified. Finally, exclusive markers of PCPNIC combined insults were also identified based on commonly deregulated proteins in all comparisons involving PCPNIC (PCPNIC vs. CT, PCPNIC vs. NIC and PCPNIC vs. PCP). For a summary of the findings, see <a href="#ijms-24-14634-f001" class="usa-link">Figure 1</a>. To further characterize the relevance of the changes in the mPFC proteome of the model of SCHZ + nicotine comorbidity during adolescence, the exclusive markers of PCPNIC mice were plotted in interactome maps. This methodological approach was adapted from Barrera-Conde and collaborators [<a href="#B60-ijms-24-14634" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B60-ijms-24-14634">60</a>].</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="ijms-24-14634-f001"><h3 class="obj_head">Figure 1.</h3>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=10572990_ijms-24-14634-g001.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/2197/10572990/7a7f8bfde318/ijms-24-14634-g001.jpg" loading="lazy" height="829" width="791" alt="Figure 1"></a></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/ijms-24-14634-f001/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p>Summary of the findings for female and male mice. Circles indicate the groups used in the comparisons. Rectangles with round corners indicate the total number of deregulated proteins for each sex in the specified comparison as well as the number of deregulated proteins that were exclusive markers of a given experimental group (PCP (phencyclidine exposure), NIC (nicotine exposure) and PCPNIC) in that same comparison. The number of upregulated and downregulated marker proteins is also shown. In the hexagons, the number of proteins that were commonly deregulated in all comparisons involving the specified group is shown. Note that the group that has the highest number of commonly deregulated proteins is the PCPNIC one. Although commonly deregulated proteins were also identified regarding the PCP group, their number was considerably smaller than what was observed for the PCPNIC group. No commonly deregulated proteins were observed regarding comparisons involving the NIC group.</p></figcaption></figure><section id="sec2dot1-ijms-24-14634"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">2.1. Proteomic Profile of the mPFC of PCP, NIC and PCPNIC Mice When Compared to Controls</h3>
<p><a href="#ijms-24-14634-f002" class="usa-link">Figure 2</a>A shows results from the proteomic analysis of the mPFC of PCP, NIC and PCPNIC mice when compared to CT. The PCPNIC group presented a higher number of deregulated proteins (males = 169, females = 156) when compared to PCP (males = 38, females = 36) and NIC (males = 134, females = 68) mice.</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="ijms-24-14634-f002"><h4 class="obj_head">Figure 2.</h4>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=10572990_ijms-24-14634-g002.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/2197/10572990/0599c0f06b33/ijms-24-14634-g002.jpg" loading="lazy" height="1069" width="791" alt="Figure 2"></a></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/ijms-24-14634-f002/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p>Proteomic profile of the mPFC of male (left) and female (right) mice exposed to phencyclidine (PCP), nicotine (NIC) or the combined insults (PCPNIC) when compared to controls (CT). (<strong>A</strong>) Venn diagrams showing deregulated proteins in NIC, PCP and PCPNIC mice when compared to CT ones. (<strong>B</strong>) Top 6 Reactome pathways of exclusively deregulated proteins in PCPNIC when compared to CT. (<strong>C</strong>) Top 6 Reactome pathways of commonly deregulated proteins in NIC, PCP and PCPNIC when compared to CT. Each color bar graph represents a specific pathway, and shades of a given color identify pathways that share a parental line. Dashed outlines represent pathways identified only once.</p></figcaption></figure><p>The analysis of PCPNIC mice also indicated a higher number of exclusively deregulated proteins (<a href="#ijms-24-14634-f002" class="usa-link">Figure 2</a>A) in both males (n = 52) and females (n = 109) when compared to exclusive proteins identified in the PCP (males = 14, females = 8) and NIC (males = 23, females = 21) groups. Among the six more frequent Reactome pathways identified in PCPNIC mice, three (signal transduction, immune system and membrane trafficking) were affected in both males and females (<a href="#ijms-24-14634-f002" class="usa-link">Figure 2</a>B). The innate immune system and signaling by Rho GTPases, Miro GTPases and RHOBTB3 were present among the other top Reactome pathways only in males. As for females, metabolism, metabolism of proteins and post-translational modification of proteins were enriched (<a href="#ijms-24-14634-f002" class="usa-link">Figure 2</a>B). Detailed analyses of the impact of nicotine and phencyclidine isolated exposures are described in <a href="#app1-ijms-24-14634" class="usa-link">Supplementary Material S2 (Figures S1–S3 and Tables S1 and S2)</a>.</p>
<p>In males, 16 proteins were commonly deregulated in PCP, NIC and PCPNIC when compared to CT mice. The analysis of females showed 14 proteins commonly deregulated (<a href="#ijms-24-14634-f002" class="usa-link">Figure 2</a>C). The Reactome analysis indicated that none of the six more frequent pathways were shared between males and females. In males, those pathways were signal transduction, metabolism, immune and neuronal systems, transmission across chemical synapses and signaling by receptor tyrosine kinases. In females, transport of small molecules, post-translational modification and metabolism of proteins and other pathways related to mitotic cell division were the most frequent ones (<a href="#ijms-24-14634-f002" class="usa-link">Figure 2</a>C).</p>
<p>A list of deregulated proteins involved in each comparison can be found in <a href="#app1-ijms-24-14634" class="usa-link">Supplementary Material S1, Sheets 3 to 8</a>.</p></section><section id="sec2dot2-ijms-24-14634"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">2.2. Proteomic Profile of the mPFC of PCP or PCPNIC Mice When Compared to Nicotine-Exposed Ones</h3>
<p>In total, in males, 114 proteins were differentially expressed in PCP and 59 proteins were differentially expressed in PCPNIC as compared to NIC mice. In females, 24 and 94 proteins were deregulated in PCP and PCPNIC, respectively (<a href="#ijms-24-14634-f003" class="usa-link">Figure 3</a>A). Proteins that were not targeted by nicotine history were those exclusively deregulated in PCP and PCPNIC as compared to NIC mice. In males, the analysis indicated 84 proteins exclusively deregulated in PCP and 29 in PCPNIC mice. In females, those numbers were 12 for PCP mice and 82 for PCPNIC ones (<a href="#ijms-24-14634-f003" class="usa-link">Figure 3</a>A).</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="ijms-24-14634-f003"><h4 class="obj_head">Figure 3.</h4>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=10572990_ijms-24-14634-g003.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/2197/10572990/f288f84fd5a4/ijms-24-14634-g003.jpg" loading="lazy" height="995" width="789" alt="Figure 3"></a></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/ijms-24-14634-f003/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p>Proteomic profile of the mPFC of male (left) and female (right) mice exposed either to phencyclidine (PCP) or to the combined insults (PCPNIC) when compared to nicotine-exposed (NIC) ones (<strong>A</strong>,<strong>B</strong>) and of PCPNIC mice when compared to PCP ones (<strong>C</strong>). (<strong>A</strong>) Venn diagrams showing deregulated proteins in PCP and PCPNIC mice when compared to NIC ones. (<strong>B</strong>) Top 6 Reactome pathways of exclusively deregulated proteins in PCPNIC when compared to NIC. (<strong>C</strong>) Top 6 Reactome pathways of deregulated proteins in PCPNIC when compared to PCP. Each color bar graph represents a specific pathway, and shades of a given color identify pathways that share a parental line. Dashed outlines represent pathways identified only once.</p></figcaption></figure><p>Regarding the exclusively deregulated proteins in PCPNIC mice (<a href="#ijms-24-14634-f003" class="usa-link">Figure 3</a>B), the functional characterization identified three out of the six top pathways enriched in both males and females (immune system and innate immune system and metabolism). When considering the other three top pathways, in males, developmental biology and hemostasis-related pathways stood out. In females, signal transduction, post-translational modification and metabolism of proteins were enriched.</p>
<p>The Reactome analysis of the exclusively deregulated proteins in PCP mice and a detailed list of the proteins involved in each comparison can be found in <a href="#app1-ijms-24-14634" class="usa-link">Supplementary Material S2</a> and in <a href="#app1-ijms-24-14634" class="usa-link">Supplementary Material S1, Sheets 9 to 12</a>.</p></section><section id="sec2dot3-ijms-24-14634"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">2.3. Proteomic Profile of the mPFC of PCPNIC Mice When Compared to PCP-Exposed Ones</h3>
<p>To further characterize proteins differentially expressed by the combined insult, we compared PCPNIC to PCP mice. The data analysis unveiled 142 and 84 proteins deregulated in males and females, respectively. Among the six more frequent Reactome pathways, four (immune system, signal transduction, metabolism and metabolism of proteins) were affected in both males and females (<a href="#ijms-24-14634-f003" class="usa-link">Figure 3</a>C), which indicates a high coincidence between sexes in response to the dual insult. The innate immune system and vesicle-mediated transport were present among the top six Reactome pathways only in males. As for females, membrane trafficking and post-translational protein modification aggregated more proteins (<a href="#ijms-24-14634-f003" class="usa-link">Figure 3</a>C). A list of proteins involved in this comparison can be found in <a href="#app1-ijms-24-14634" class="usa-link">Supplementary Material S1, Sheets 13 and 14</a>.</p></section><section id="sec2dot4-ijms-24-14634"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">2.4. Protein Markers in the mPFC of PCPNIC Mice</h3>
<p>To identify specific protein markers of the SCHZ + nicotine comorbidity adolescent model, the proteins exclusively deregulated in the comparison between PCPNIC and CT (males = 52, females = 109) in PCPNIC when compared to NIC (males = 29, females = 82) and in PCPNIC when compared to PCP mice (males: n = 142, females: n = 84) were plotted in Venn diagrams. In males, 16 commonly deregulated proteins (protein markers of PCPNIC) were identified, while in females, there were 45 proteins (<a href="#ijms-24-14634-f004" class="usa-link">Figure 4</a>A). The functional characterization of these proteins indicated that none of the six top pathways were common for males and females. In males, the top enriched pathways were innate immune system, developmental biology, axon guidance, nervous system development, platelet activation and MAPK family signaling cascades (<a href="#ijms-24-14634-f004" class="usa-link">Figure 4</a>B). As for females, metabolism, post-translational protein modification and metabolism, signal transduction, immune system and adaptative immune system stood out (<a href="#ijms-24-14634-f004" class="usa-link">Figure 4</a>B). The Reactome analysis of the protein markers of either PCP or nicotine exposure and a detailed list of the proteins involved can be found in <a href="#app1-ijms-24-14634" class="usa-link">Supplementary Material S1, Sheets 13 to 18</a>, and in <a href="#app1-ijms-24-14634" class="usa-link">Supplementary Material S2</a>.</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="ijms-24-14634-f004"><h4 class="obj_head">Figure 4.</h4>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=10572990_ijms-24-14634-g004.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/2197/10572990/8a7384a08901/ijms-24-14634-g004.jpg" loading="lazy" height="716" width="788" alt="Figure 4"></a></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/ijms-24-14634-f004/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p>Proteomic profile of the mPFC of male (left) and female (right) mice exposed to the combined insults (PCPNIC) when compared to mice exposed to phencyclidine (PCP), mice exposed to nicotine (NIC) or the control condition (CT). (<strong>A</strong>) Venn diagrams showing deregulated proteins in PCPNIC mice when compared to CT, NIC and PCP ones. (<strong>B</strong>) Top 6 Reactome pathways of commonly deregulated proteins in PCPNIC when compared to CT, NIC and PCP ones. Each color bar graph represents a specific pathway, and shades of a given color identify pathways that share a parental line. Dashed outlines represent pathways identified only once.</p></figcaption></figure><p>These data show a large number of protein markers of PCPNIC combined insults. To further understand the relevance of changes in the mPFC proteome of mice modeling the comorbidity during adolescence, we created interactome maps for males and females using these proteins (<a href="#ijms-24-14634-f005" class="usa-link">Figure 5</a>). This approach allowed the identification of subnetworks. In order to facilitate the description and discussion of the results, each subnetwork was named in accordance with its main identified biological function (<a href="#ijms-24-14634-t001" class="usa-link">Table 1</a> and <a href="#ijms-24-14634-t002" class="usa-link">Table 2</a>).</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="ijms-24-14634-f005"><h4 class="obj_head">Figure 5.</h4>

<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/ijms-24-14634-f005/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p>Interactome map showing protein markers of PCPNIC male (<strong>A</strong>) and female (<strong>B</strong>) mPFC. Proteins were grouped into different subnetworks so that each color represents one subnetwork. Complete lists of these proteins, organized in subnetworks, are presented in <a href="#ijms-24-14634-t001" class="usa-link">Table 1</a> and <a href="#ijms-24-14634-t002" class="usa-link">Table 2</a>.</p></figcaption></figure><section class="tw xbox font-sm" id="ijms-24-14634-t001"><h4 class="obj_head">Table 1.</h4>
